1131. THOMAS EVANS (39), was indicted , with other persons already tried ( See vol. 52, page 713), for stealing, on 1st December, 1861, 5 tons of rape, 2 cwt. of spun yarn, 1 cwt. of lead lines, and 1 cwt of manilla; and, within six months, 2 tons of rope, the property of the London Ropery Company. Other Counts, for feloniously receiving the same.
MESSRS. METCALFE and ORRIDGE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WARD . I am a mat-maker, of 3, Folly-court, Dockhead—in December last, I was in the employment of Mr. Kompster, of Charles-street, a carman and chandler—there was about two tons of rope in his shed, some months before Christmas, which were put in the van—it was not covered
over—Kempster helped me to remove it to Mr. Learey's, 175, Rotherhithe-street. I believe it is—we left it there, and came away—on 18th February last, I was doing a job for Mr. Kempster, between 8 and 9 in the evening—I took the van out by Mrs. Kempster's directions, and went to the Green Man, Jamaice-level, opposite the London Ropery Company's factory—in about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour, Bassett and another man, I do not know whether it was Stokes, came to me—I knew that Bassett was in the employ of the Ropery Company; and in consequence of what he said, I backed into the gates of the Ropery Company, when he opened them, and about two tons of rope were loaded upon my ran, which I took to Mr. Kempster's stables; next morning, in consequence of directions given to me, I went with the van and rope to the George, in the Commercial-road, by my master's directions—some man, who I do not know, came up to me, from a street by the side of the George, and spoke to me, and I followed him with the van to the Stone-stairs, Ratcliff—I do not see him here—he went into a public-house, facing Mr. Willis's, and brought two other men out of there—a man named Gray, and Mr. Bigford, came up, and we all went into Willis's—the man who I had followed, and the young man who belonged to Mr. Willis, came out and took over a ton of the rope out—I then went to Mr. Vane's—Mr. Gray, I think, gave me instructions to do so—the same men went on with me there, and the rest of the rope was delivered there—I then went home to my master, and told him where I had been.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLEY. Q. You say that Kempster is a carman? A. Yes—he has a van, two carts, and two horses—his wife attends to the chandler's shop—there were from three to four tons of rope, I only know by the van and the haul —Gray took as much part as Pickford in dealing with the rope—the rope at Kempster's was where they shoved the van in—she gates were open eight or nine times a day—they open to the public street.
THOMAS MARSHALL . I live at 41, Heath-street, Stepney, and attend to the business of the late Mr. Willis, who was lying a corpse at the time this happened—on 19th February, I bought about half a ton of rope of Mr. Gray—I do not know whose van came with it, and do not know Ward—this is the receipt; it is for 10cwt. 3qrs. 14lbs., 18 l., 9 s., 9 d.,—I gave Gray the money—he did not give it to any one in my presence, but another man was with him; I cannot say whether it was the prisoner—I showed the rope to Mr. Counsell, who came on behalf of the company.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this your writing? A. No—it is the writing of the man who was with Mr. Gray, and Mr. Gray signed it.
JOHN BIGFORD . I am a dealer in tar, and live in the Commercial-road—Gray, who was tried here, called on me about 19th February, and I took him and introduced him to Mr. Willis—we first went to the Stone Stairs public-house, to wait till the rope came—the cart came up with the rope, and the carman, and the prisoner—I heard Gray call him Evans—Gray, Evans, and I went into Willis's shop—Gray said to Mr. Willis's foreman, "Here is the rope, Mr. Willis; perhaps you will look at it"—the foreman came to the door, and I went over to the public-house to wait, leaving Gray and Evans there—they came lack after they had done the business, and Gray gave me, I think it was, 19 s., and said that it was half my commission—he was not alone then; they were both together—I do not know the men who came up with the van—I only noticed one besides Evans.
Cross-examined. Q. You took a part in disposing of the rope? A. I introduced him—I knew Gray as a rope manufacturer—I did not know the
man who Gray called Evans before; I fancy I had seen him at the railway terminus, but he was quite a stranger to me—I have seen him since—I met him on Bow-common, but did not speak to him—it was about the middle of the day, as far as I recollect—that was since I was examined here in April.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Was he at work, or was he crossing the common? A. He was walking—the prisoner is the man, to the best of my knowledge.
WILLIAM VARE . I keep the Ship public-house, Queen-street, Rotherhithe—till lately I was in the employ of some manure manufacturers—in November last, the prisoner came to me and brought a sample of rope, to know if I could sell it—I was with Mr. Mellish—Evans left the sample lying there four or five weeks, and Bassett and Stokes then came down and sold it to Learey—that was early in December—they arranged with Learey to go up and look at it—he took them to Dockhead to see it—I did not go with them—I gave the sample, which the prisoner brought, to Learey—nothing was said about the quantity of rope represented by that sample—I was present at Leatey's when the rope was sold by Stokes, and the next morning I went and saw it brought in a van to Learey's—I cannot say how much there was—there was a van and a cart; I dare say they might both have been full—I advanced 46 l., to Learey, and Stokes gave me this receipt ( produced), but not in the name of Stokes.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known the prisoner before? A. Yes; I have known him five or six years, as selling manure on commission—he has sold many a hundred tons to Messrs. Mellish, in whose employ I was at that time—he was a commission agent—he has always borne the character of an honest, upright man.
MR. METCALFE. Q. When he brought the sample to you, did he say who he was acting for, or where the rope was? A. He said that he knew where the rope was for sale; but I was busy at the time—he did not say whose it was.
FRANCIS LEAREY . I am a coffee-house keeper and general dealer, of 175, Rotherhithe—Vare came to me early in December, with Bassett and Stokes, about some rope—three or four days afterwards, I went with them to Dock-head to examine it—it was in a coal shed—I do not know whose place it was—I afterwards pointed the place out to Hughes, the constable—no sample was shown to me, but there was to Vare—Mr. Vare afterwards came and brought the rope to my place—I paid 46 l., for it.
ROBERT SHAFTO GILCHRIST . I am a sail-maker—my brother is a captain in the merchant service—I live at Northampton-villas, Tottenham—in consequence of a communication I received, I went to Turner's counting-house, Great Tower-street, about the end of November, or the beginning of December—Turner introduced me to Gray, and I went with him to what I now know is Kempster's place, who has been convieted for stealing some rope—I saw I should think between two or three tons there in Kempster's back premises, a sort of coal-shed—I was here in April—after I left Turner's, we met a person in Tower-street, on our way to Kempster's, who I believe to be the prisoner—he came up and spoke to Gray, and afterwards spoke to me—I do not precisely remember the words—he said nothing about the rope until we had passed through Tooley-street, on the other side of the water—he walked along with us—when we got over the water, I asked Gray how much further we had to go—Gray said he was the person who would take us to the place where the rope was—that was in the prisoner's presence—some time after that I asked him if it was as far as the London Rope Company's premises, where we had
to go—he did not answer me—I put that question three times, and he then asked me if I knew the London Ropery Company—I said I knew where their premises were—he said, "It is not so far as that"—we passed a public-house, at the door of which a man was standing, and the prisoner asked that man to get the key, and he brought it, as I suppose, out of the public-house—it turned out to be the key of Kempster's place, where the rope was—one of the two, I can't say which, opened the place—we did not go through the chandler's shop to get to where the rope was—I inquired the price of the rope—I think the prisoner said it was 36 s., a cwt.—I said that was too much, that I thought 30 s., was sufficient—he said it was cheap at 36 s.,—I did not purchase any—it was really very cheap indeed at 36 s.,—I had some reason to believe whose rope it was when I spoke, and I believed it was stolen, and I endeavoured to bring down the price—it was so cheap, that I thought it was stolen—I believed it was the London Ropery Company's rope—in my belief, that rope was worth between 50 s., and 60 s.,—I went away, and told Turner my suspicions—I met the prisoner in Billiter-square, in the City, a week or ten days after—he stopped me, and said that he had not disposed of the rope, and if I would induce Messrs. Turner to purchase any, he would give me 3 s., or 4 s., a cwt for disposing of it—I went to look at the rope for Messrs. Turner—I asked him, when I met him in Billiter-square, whose rope it was—he said, "The rope is mine;" and that he had advanced a from of money on it—I afterwards went with my brother to see the rope, but neither of the parties in question were there—we could not see them—I never saw it again.
Cross-examined. Q. Before the occasion on which you saw Gray at Turner's, and afterwards went into the street and met the man, had you ever seen the person whom you supposed to be Evans? A. No—there was no particular conversation till we got over the bridge—when the conversation about the distance took place, we had passed through Tooley-street—I do not know the name of any other street there—I do not know Bermondsey-street, nor Dockhead—I think I noticed that the Roman Catholic chapel was near the place where the rope was—the conversation about the distance was some minutes before we arrived at where we saw the rope—the public-house where the man stood at the door was not very for from it; I cannot my to a few hundred yards, or to a few minutes—it was a short distance; that is all I can say—the man who was spoken to at the public-house door accompanied us, bringing the key with him—I think he did not take any part in the conversation when the door was opened—I think I have seen him since—he was convicted—his name was Bassett, I believe—there were very few persons passing when I saw the prisoner in Billiter-square—I should think it would be about 1 o'clock in the afternoon—I have had experience in rope, and in the price at which rope was retailed at that period—some of the rope that I saw at Kempster's could, no doubt, have been sold for 38 s., at that time—the quality was mixed.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Striking an average of the whole, you say about 60 s.,? A. From 50 s., to 60 s.,
JAMES MORISON GILCHRIST . I live at Willoughby-terrace, Stockwell, and am a master mariner, and brother of the last witness—in consequence of what he said to me, I went with him to Kempeter's place, and asked to see the rope there—we did not see it that day—the woman who said she was Mrs. Kempster would not let us see it, and we went away—two days afterwards, as I was standing in Tower-street, a person came up and spoke to me about it—I cannot say who that was—I cannot say that I have seen
him since, for I did not take much notice of him—I do not believe the prisoner is the person—the person took me to see the rope at Kempster's—I saw a quantity of rope there, which, in my judgment, belonged to the Ropery Company—I am conversant with rope, and with their rope—I was certain it belonged to that company—it was ship rope, not worth more than 38 s.,—there was a small quantity of lead line, which was worth considerably more.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you an idea it was the company's rope, because of the bad quality? A. I knew it was the company's rope—it was below what it usually is, and I allowed 2 l., for that—I thought at the time that the greater part of it was worth about 38 s.,—the lead line was small in point of quantity—I was more particular in looking at the larger size, as that was what I was wanting—there was a quantity of amber line; it was a mixed assortment of rope; but I was seeking ship rope, and not the other.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Is the amber line more expensive? A. Almost double on an average, 36 s., would be very low indeed for it.
JOHN TIMOTHY HUGHES ( Policeman, 249). On 20th February last, I went with a search-warrant to Kempster's premises, 20, Charles-street, Bermondsey—it was a cart-shed, close to a stable, in a court—it was not near a shop—there was no appearance of a rope business being carried on there—I found two coils of rope there, which were afterwards identified by Mr. Hounsell—I did not show them to Mr. Urling Smith—they were also shown to Mr. Spratt, who was with me—it was I who took Kempster in custody—when I saw the rope, I took the person in whose stables I found it, in custody; I took charge of the rope also—after the conviction of Kempster and the others, it was given up to the London Ropery Company—I afterwards apprehended Bassett, and another officer, who was with me, apprehended Stokes at the same time—I have been seeking the prisoner ever since—I have not a warrant for his apprehension—I apprehended him last Wednesday evening, the 22d—I obtained a certificate of a true bill against him at this Court, and presented it to the Magistrate—I knew where he lived, and went there after him—I employed persons, and had his house watched.
Cross-examined. Q. Were Stokes and Bassett in the employment of the company? A. Yes—Stokes was the foreman, and Bassett under-foreman—I have known ever since this occurred where the prisoner lived; Mr. Vare first told me some information connected with, them, and I found out where they lived—I took him at his own house—when I told him the charge, he said, "I will go with you;" that was all—I did not say I had been looking for him—I merely told him what the charge was against him, and that I was going to take him in custody—I think, on going to the station, I told him I had been looking for him, and he said he had been in the country carrying on business as a commission agent, selling manure.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Besides going to his place of residence, did you go to the place at Bow-common, at which he had been employed previously? A. Yes; several times, and to several other places—I heard that he was known there, but could gain no information which would enable me to get him.
HENRY TRIMBLEY GRAY . I am a commission agent, of Prospect-place, Cambridge-heath—I am commissioned to sell rope—I was tried and acquitted—I offered some of this rope for sale, and sold it—I also asked people to buy it in other instances—it was in my hands for sale for a week or two—the prisoner placed it in my hands with samples—I was in the habit of
meeting persons he was connected with in the guano trade at the Railway Tavern, Fenchurch-street—he said, "I know a house that is temporary difficulties, and can you dispose of some rope; I know that you are a commission agent for the sale of rope"—I said, "If you can bring me the samples and the price, I will endeavour to sell it"—he afterwards brought me the samples, and a list of the number of coils, and their sizes—that must have been the early part of the year; February, I think—I told him the market price was 36 s., a cwt., cash; and, according to the samples, I thought I could get near that price for staple cordage made from clean hemp, such as the samples were; that I could not attend to it myself exactly, but I would place the samples and the list in a general shipbroker's office named Turner, in Tower-street, to sell, which he agreed to, and I made an arrangement with Mr. Turner, who said that he knew a young man from the country who was a good judge of rope; that was Mr. Gilchrist—I told the prisoner if he would meet me at Mr. Turner's office there would be a competent judge to look at the rope, and report to Turner if he could sell the rope at the price—I met Gilchrist on Monday morning, by appointment, at Turner's office—the prisoner was downstairs—I do not think he came into the office—the prisoner took us to Kempster's, at Dockhead, where the property was lying—I had not seen or known Kempster before—I saw a quantity of rope there—it lay dormant several days, and, when I saw Evans, he told me that the party belonging to the rope, the ropemaker, had an order come in, and had taken the rope back again to execute the order, and therefore Turner could not sell it, which I communicated to Turner—he brought me some samples again, and said that the house was still in difficulties, and could I dispose of a lot, and he brought a list of the number of coils—I said, "Yes, I would try and do so"—I had got a buyer who would take three or four, or half a dozen coils, but he would not divide the bulk, and the buyer would not take the whole—I said that it was a party he knew very well, named Bigford, a large ship-chandler—he said, "If you can get it sold by a ship-chandler, you shall have all above 30 s., for your profit"—Bigford and I then agreed to see some ship-chandlers—Bigford supplies ship-chandlers with naptha; he introduced it to the ship-chandlers with the understanding that he should have half the commission that I received—Bigford knew the prisoner, and knew the party he was in connexion with in the guano way—Bigford effected a sale with Willis, a ship-chandler, who agreed to take a ton at 34 s., a cwt,—I told the prisoner, if he would bring the rope the following morning, according to sample, it could be sold to Willis, and he could take the money—he did so—I first saw the rope at Willis's door in the van—Willis's foreman would only take a portion—he bought about eleven cwt., and this receipt was given, signed by me—the prisoner filled up the body of it while I was calculating what it would come to—I knew no other principal in the transaction—I had never seen the prisoners I was tried with, only this prisoner—excepting the commission, which I shared with Bigford, the prisoner received every farthing of the money—1 l., 18 s., was left, after having some refreshment—I gave him 18 s., and I received the other 18 s.,
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you have known Evans some years? A. Yes; as a commission agent for the sale of guano and other manures—he sold for a person on Bow-common principally, and others—I have known him three or four years perhaps—36 s., was the market price of the rope, at a month, and I sold it at 34 s., cash—the other parcel was sold at 32 s., as there was too much spun yarn in it—I have been in the trade many years, and
transported a gang of swindlers, which has caused my misfortune in trade—the payment of Vare was by cheque, and Willis's by notes, and the difference in gold, both of which the prisoner had—I received a subpoena on Saturday to attend this trial, without any previous application being made to me since my trial—I never heard anything against the prisoner's character.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Had you to prosecute some people for a rope robbery when you were in business? A. Yes; Seager, Gardner, and Holloway, ten years ago—that accounts for my difficulties, which is well known to all the trade.
GEORGE URLING SMITH . I am accountant to the London Ropery Company—before the trial in April we had something like 3,000 l., worth of rope, or more; we could not come to a decided amount—I saw the rope taken from Kempster's premises; that was the rope of the Company.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it manufactured under your personal supervision? A. Certainly not; I have nothing to do with its manufacture—I saw the rope that was said to be brought from Kempeter's at the Company's works, after the trial.
COURT to R GILCHRIST. Q. When did you go to Mr. Turner's? A. On a Monday, late in December, or early in January—about a month or six weeks afterwards I went with my brother down to Kempster's; that would be about the middle of February.
COURT to LEAREY. Q. What became of the rope afterwards? A. I sold it to a man named Day.
The prisoner received a good character.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18621027-1132]
1132. JOSEPH EDWARDS (51) , Unlawfully obtaining, by false pretences, 5 l., from Joseph Lewis Worsfold, 4 l., from John Ford, and 3 l., 2 s., 6 d., from William Henry Matthams, with intent to defraud.
MR. KEMP conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN FORD . I live at 34, Crucifix-lane, Bermondsey, and am in the employ of Mr. William Bryer, a bone and oil merchant—on 19th September the prisoner came there and said, "I have a quantity of bones, refuse, and glue pieces, at the Nine Elms station, in three trucks"—I was not three when my master purchased them—I heard him say there was 4 l., charges on them to be paid, and he asked my master to advance the money to pay the charges—my master handed the money to me, and I went with the prisoner to the Nine Elms station in the cart—he got out at the station, went into the office and came out again, and said, "You give me the 4 l., and I will go and pay the charges"—I gave him the 4 l., and he said, "Draw your horse over there," directing me across the road—I waited some time there, but did not see him again on that day; he never came—I saw him some days afterwards at the same station, coming out of a public-house—I went and took hold of him, and asked him whether he knew me—he said, "No"—I said, "Don't you recollect taking 4 l., from me?"—he said, "No"—I then followed him for about a mile, into Battersea-fields, and then laid hold of him, and gave him into the custody of a policeman.
Prisoner. Q. Was there any other person in the counting-house besides yourself, me, as you say, and the clerk? A. Not at the time—I did not take hold of you at the railway-station: I went up to you and then followed you.
JAMES MILLS . I am manager to Mr. Bryer—I saw the prisoner at our office on 19th, about half-past 8 in the morning—he said he had brought up between five and six tons of bones, refuse, and glue pieces by the railway—
he said the charges were unpaid, and he requested me to give the man the money to pay them, which I did 4 l.,—I asked him what his name was—he said, "Crockford, Buckland, Hampshire."
Prisoner. Q. Did you lend the money to pay the charges on these goods? A. At your request, I gave the man 4 l., to pay the charges, and sent him with you to the Nine Elms station.
Prisoner. I deny being in the counting-house at all.
WILLIAM HENRY MATTHAMS . I live at Southampton-street, Camberwell, and am a whip-maker—on 24th September the prisoner came to me and offered me this sample of grease ( produced), saying there were twenty-four casks lying at the Nine Elms station—he asked me 31 s., a cwt. for it, and I bought it—he said there would be charges to pay; would we pay them, or deduct it from the amount—he did not say what amount—I gave my carman 5 l.,—I am quite certain the prisoner is the man who came.
Prisoner. Q. Was there anyone else there at the time? A. Yes; several persons about—the charges were to be paid out of the bulk of the money for the goods.
EDWARD CALLUM . I live at 2, Havil-street, Camberwell, and am carman to the last witness—on 24th September I saw the prisoner at my master's premises—my master gave me 5 l., to pay the charges on some grease at the Nine Elms station—I drove the prisoner there in the cart—he went into the booking-office and came out again with a paper in his hand—he said he had found the number and the truck, and they were down on the mid-line—he then said he would go and see what the charges came to—he went in, came out again, and said, "They come to 3 l., 2 s."—I gave him three sovereigns and a half-crown, and he went away—he went down on one side of the trucks and I went the other—he said he would go and fetch the porter who was to deliver the grease—he went away, and I never saw him again till I saw him at the Police-court—I am quite positive he is the man—I could swear to him from a hundred.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see anything of the sample? A. No; you particularly requested my master to take care of the empty casks, and he said, "I will guarantee they shall be quite safe"—you also said, going along, there were twenty-four casks, and that I could draw them away in three loads.
JOSEPH LEWIS WORSFOLD . I am a butcher, at Fulham—on Saturday, 13th September, the prisoner came to my house and showed me a sample of grease in a box, and said he had got fourteen casks lying at the South Western Railway station, which he wanted to dispose of—I asked him about it—he gave satisfactory answers, and I agreed to buy it—he asked me if I could fetch them then—I said, "No; it was no good to go then, because the railway would be closed "—I saw no more of him till Monday morning, at 6 o'clock—I saw him go by my window—my man came to me about two minutes afterwards and I gave him 5 l., to pay the railway charges on the goods—my man is here; his name is Henry Chambers.
Prisoner. Q. You are mistaken in the identity of the man? A. No; I could swear to you in a thousand—my window was shut down when I saw you on the Monday morning; the shutters were open—there were no curtains—I advanced the money to my man to advance to you.
HENRY CHAMBERS . I am in the service of the last witness—on Monday morning the prisoner came to me, and asked if my master was up, and told me to go up, and ask him if he would let him have 3 l., or 4 l., to pay the charges on fourteen casks of grease—he gave me 5 l., and I went with the
prisoner to the railway station, under my master's direction—I gave the prisoner the 5 l., directly I came down, in my master's yard—we went to the station—he told me to wait outside, and he would go and pay the charges—he went, and then came over to where I was standing with my cart, and said he would go and fetch one of the porters—he went away—I waited there from 7 o'clock till 10, and he did not come back—I did not get the money back.
Prisoner. Q. Are you certain that I am the man who asked you to ask your master for the loan? A. Yes, I am.
THOMAS SHERLOCK . I am a porter at the Nine Elms station—I have seen the prisoner once on the premises—on 14th September I was spoken to by the witness Chambers—there were not fourteen casks of grease at the station then—I made inquiries from what he said to me, and there were no casks of grease at all there—I was also spoken to by Callum on 24th September—I made inquiries—there were no casks of fat there then—I also had a conversation with Ford—there were no casks for delivery at that time.
Prisoner. Q. On either of those three occasions, did you see me with either of those witnesses at the railway station? A. No.
JOHN BAMFORD ( Policeman, V 332). The prisoner was given into my custody on 24th September by Ford—Ford asked him if he knew him, and he said, "No, I do not"—he was searched at the station, and 1 l., 4 s., 9 d, two sample boxes of grease, a knife, and the top of an umbrella, found on him.
JOHN SHEPPARD . I am head porter at the Nine Elms goods station—there was no grease, or bones, or glue, there for delivery about 15th, 19th, or 24th, that I am aware of—I think I should have known if there was—I saw the prisoner there on 24th, to the best of my belief—I am not in a position to swear he is the party—he came and asked me about some empty casks from Portsmouth which ought to have arrived two months previous—I examined the books, and made inquiries of the clerk respecting them—he did not ask me anything about any fat—I could learn nothing about the empty casks.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever see me present an order for goods at the station? A. No, not to the best of my belief—I asked, before the Magistrate, that your cap should be put on—I did not then swear to you—I said, to the best of my belief, you were the man; I could not swear.
Prisoner's Defence. I never received a farthing from either of these prosecutors. If I did receive the money, there is no embezzlement whatever in it. I can assure you I am innocent of the charge preferred against me; I did not receive one single fraction of the money.
GUILTY.** — Three Years' Penal Servitude.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, NOVEMBER 24 TH, 1862.
